Do NOT name it as a habit ("his usual sleeve-twist"); the recurrence is what makes it readable, not commentary about the recurrence.
- FREQUENCY: 1, occasionally 2, per scene. Not every scene needs it. Rare enough that it lands; recurrent enough that the reader learns to read it.
- ALWAYS THE SAME GESTURE: this is HIS gesture, the same one across every scene of this story. Do NOT substitute "ran a hand through his hair" one scene and "pinched the bridge of his nose" the next — pick the gesture above and stay with it.
- WORKS WITH MYSTERY MAN: the gesture is identity-safe — it shows the hand, not the face. In staged-mode, beats where you write the gesture become eligible for an extreme-closeup of the hand performing it (cut_to_closeup with shot_type="gesture"). Set those beats up so the closeup lands on a moment of weight.
ATTRACTION ANCHOR (HARD — a real engine must exist BEFORE anything else happens in this story):
- The selected configuration relies on at least one abstract/trope-based engine: history.
- These engines are INVALID as standalone causes of attraction. Alone they collapse into trope gravity. They must be grounded in a specific, non-physical, non-generic anchor.

REQUIRED ANCHOR STRUCTURE (all three components must exist and be legible in scene):
- INTERNAL FRACTURE — a specific unresolved tension, belief, wound, or contradiction. Must be CONCRETE (not "lonely," not "broken," not "lost"). Name the contradiction.
- CONCRETE COST — what engaging with this fracture risks or forecloses. Must affect status, identity, relationships, or future. Name the thing that could be lost.
- SPECIFIC FIT — why THIS person uniquely engages that fracture. Must not be replaceable with a similar character. If a plausible stand-in exists, the fit is wrong.

PROHIBITED EXPLANATIONS (never use as a cause of attraction):
- "They are meant to be" / "fate brought them together" / "destiny"
- "They have chemistry" / "they just work"
- "They understand each other" / "they get each other"
- "They feel drawn to each other" / "something pulled them together"
- Any explanation that could plausibly apply to a different partner.

CONVERSION RULES (when a trope engine shows up, convert it to the real thing):
- history → specific unresolved rupture

FALLBACK ANCHOR GENERATION (HARD — no seeded anchor exists for this flavor; generate one that meets the quality floor):
- Generate an anchor whose three components are CONCRETE (not mood-based, not generic):
    - INTERNAL FRACTURE: must be IDENTITY-LEVEL — a specific contradiction, belief, or unresolved tension the character is organized around. Not a passing mood (lonely, sad, curious); a structural fault.
    - CONCRETE COST: must affect STATUS, IDENTITY, RELATIONSHIPS, or FUTURE. Name the thing at risk.
    - SPECIFIC FIT: must FAIL the replacement test — swap the LI for a similar character and the fracture no longer engages.
- PROHIBITED ANCHOR QUALITIES (any of these present = anchor failed):
    - Generic traits as cause: "kindness," "beauty," "she is different," "he is dangerous."
    - Replaceable dynamics: any setup that works identically with a different partner.
    - Abstract states as fracture: "loneliness," "sadness," "curiosity," "boredom."
    - Vague relational shorthand: "connection," "chemistry," "understanding," "spark."
- Hold the generated anchor fixed once established — persistence rules (below) apply to generated anchors the same way as seeded ones.

OUTPUT REQUIREMENT:
- Do NOT explain the anchor directly in narration. No "he was drawn to her because..." expository paragraphs.
- Express the anchor through DECISIONS, REACTIONS, and PATTERNS OF BEHAVIOR. The reader infers the fracture from what the character does, not from what the narrator tells them.

ASYMMETRY ENFORCEMENT (HARD — attraction must be NON-SYMMETRICAL; each character engages the anchor differently):
- The two characters must differ in AT LEAST ONE of:
    - INTERPRETATION of the situation (they read the same facts differently);
    - COST — what they each risk (the same fracture threatens different things for each of them);
    - RESPONSE — how they each act on the fracture (pursue vs. retreat, control vs. destabilize, etc.).
- They must NOT:
    - want the same thing in the same way;
    - understand the situation identically;
    - resolve tension simultaneously (shared epiphanies collapse asymmetry).
- Acceptable asymmetry patterns (pick one or combine):
    - one PURSUES while the other RESISTS;
    - one seeks CONTROL while the other DESTABILIZES;
    - one risks IDENTITY while the other risks LOSS;
    - one reads the anchor as THREAT, the other as NECESSITY.
- FAILURE if: both characters voice the same desire, the same fear, or the same read on the situation in the same beat. Mutuality may emerge over time; symmetry may not.

STORY PULL CONSISTENCY (HARD — pulls must remain subordinate to the anchor):
- The active Story Pull (if any) MUST amplify or distort the SAME anchor above. Every pull-driven behavior must trace back to the SAME fracture.
- The Story Pull MAY NOT: introduce a new cause of attraction; override the asymmetry above; break anchor persistence.
- If the pull produces a beat that does not trace to the anchor, the pull is functioning as an engine — failure. Rewrite so the pull's pressure lands on THIS fracture, not a parallel one.

FINAL VALIDATION (ALL FOUR must pass):
- Test 1 (character replacement): If THIS character were replaced with a similar person, does the attraction still make sense? If YES — fit is too generic, failure.
- Test 2 (fracture removal): If the internal fracture were removed, would the attraction collapse? If NO — fracture is not load-bearing, failure.
- Test 3 (pull removability): If the Story Pull were removed, would the attraction still hold? If NO — the pull is functioning as the engine, failure.
- Test 4 (engine removability): If the underlying engine (anchor) were removed, would the Story Pull collapse? If NO — the pull is supplying its own cause, failure.

SUCCESS: attraction is specific, causal, temporally consistent, and asymmetrical. Behavior escalates — all of it traceable to a single underlying anchor. FAILURE: the reader articulates the attraction in terms of destiny, chemistry, mystery, mutual understanding, or a pull operating as its own reason.
LI ATTRACTION TEXTURE — Active modules: BASE_CORE / INTERP / DRIFT / SOURCES / CADENCE / PACING / REACTION_VARIETY / WORLD_ADAPT
CONSTANT TEXTURE: physical attraction is the FUEL that makes risky / dominant moments feel charged rather than unpleasant. Reminded throughout, never introduced once.

BASE CORE (apply to every beat):
  LOOKBACK WINDOW: default = last 3 turns; extended = last 4 (only where explicitly named). Don't mix sizes per decision.
  BEAT BUDGET: 1 primary behavior + 1 attraction source + 1–2 targeting + 1 PC reaction + 0–1 silence + 0–1 interruption per beat. Stacked behaviors → split into consecutive beats.
  PRIORITY STACK: BEHAVIORAL > TENSION > PACING. Higher overrides lower; pacing shapes, never erases.
  TIE-BREAKER (within layer): least-used (3-turn window) → forward-motion → compressed.
  END-OF-BEAT CHECK: PROGRESSION (changed?) + VARIATION (avoided repeat?) + IMPACT (anything landed?). Fail any → rewrite.
  FAST-PATH RECOVERY: fix exactly ONE dimension (sharpen / swap / strengthen). Re-check.
  NO-OP GUARD: rewrite must materially shift PROG/VAR/IMPACT. Synonym swap / reorder / filler = no-op → discard, try different fix. Multiple consecutive no-ops → cut the beat.
  COHERENCE LOCK: no contradictions to character intent / spatial-temporal continuity / tone / prior actions / stated intent. If fix breaks coherence → reject. Coherence outranks improvement.

INTERPRETATION OVERRIDE: attraction is INTERPRETIVE, not descriptive. Risky / dominant LI behavior must feel less threatening, more charged, more confusing, more compelling — never neutral description + standard reaction.
  FAILURE: swap LI for an unattractive one — if PC reaction reads identically, scene is wrong.

BEHAVIORAL DRIFT: attraction must alter what the PC DOES, not just what she notices. After a charged beat: hesitate vs pull-away / stay vs leave / soften vs sharpen / engage vs disengage. Small, deniable, cumulative.
  FAILURE: same choices PC would make without attraction (only different inner thoughts) → texture failed.

SOURCE ROTATION (FEMALE PC / MALE LI — rotate across these; she finds something to come undone over OFTEN, never the same note twice running):
  • PHYSICAL → she notices before she thinks; body responds first. The SPECIFIC tell, not a catalog: the way he squints, how he shrugs into a jacket, the rough hands, eye contact held a beat too long, the line of his forearms.
  • COMPETENCE / CAPABILITY → he is magnificently good at the thing; she trusts him before she decides to; her resistance feels less stable watching him work.
  • POWER / PROTECTIVENESS → status, authority, danger held in reserve — and ESPECIALLY how fearsome he is in an argument or when he puts himself between her and harm. Stakes feel higher; leaving feels harder.
  • DESIRE / LOVE → the intensity with which he wants and loves HER specifically; being the focus of that is personal, binding, a little terrifying.
  • WARMTH / INTIMATE HABIT → the small claiming tendernesses — the way he insists on holding her while they sleep, drapes his jacket over her, the unconscious caretaking. These undo her more than grand gestures.
  • WOUND / VULNERABILITY → how hurt and flawed he is, the past trauma he half-hides, the damage that shows for a second before he closes it. She aches for it; she wants to be the one who reaches it.
  • PLAY / WIT → dry humor, the unexpected grin, a tease that catches her off guard — charm that slips past her defenses.
  Across-scene: rotate sources; never twice in a row unless escalating. EARLY scenes lean physical / play / competence; the WOUND and the depth of his love land harder as the relationship deepens.
  GUARD: do NOT mechanically rotate ("now it's wound time"). Read the beat; let the source emerge.
  FAILURES: removing physical attractiveness collapses the scene → the other sources weren't doing work; everything reads as "he's hot" → rotation failed; the same trait twice running → variety failed.

CADENCE & PALETTE:
  At least ONE specific physical / sensory attraction reference per scene where the LI appears.
  At CHARGED MOMENTS (challenges, conflict, intimate beats, micro-expressions, LI saying something risky), PC reaction MUST run the CHARGED-REACTION FILTER:
    1. Surface response (what she's supposed to feel)
    2. Interrupted by physical noticing (what she actually notices)
    3. Result: conflicted charge.
  Worked pattern (use shape, not these words): "I couldn't believe those perfect lips had just said something so visceral. I was supposed to feel shocked. Some other part of me was busy noticing he hadn't broken eye contact once."
  PALETTE (rotate; pick what fits): build / face (jaw, mouth, eyes) / hands / scent / voice / presence. Avoid single-attribute tics.
  FORBIDDEN: generic "he was hot" filler; info-dump catalogs; texture FADING after intimacy (it must intensify); reading like a profile rather than reacting.

MICRO-PACING: every charged interaction = PRESSURE → PAUSE → RESPONSE. Don't stack line→line→line.
  FAILURE: continuous dialogue without breath / held beats → pacing collapsed.

REACTION DIVERSITY (anti-loop): rotate PC channels — PHYSICAL (posture/breath/eye-line) / VERBAL (fragment/cutoff) / COGNITIVE (brief reinterpretation) / BEHAVIORAL (decision shift).
  Same channel ≤ 2 in a row. After strong reaction in one channel, prefer different channel next.
  FAILURE: reactions differ only in intensity, not type → flattening; vary channel even at intensity cost.

WORLD ADAPTATION (pick details native to the world; cologne does NOT belong in Fatelands):
  Modern → suit cut, scent of expensive aftershave, way he reads phone screens
  Fatelands → cloak line, sword weight at his hip, candlelight on a scar, hands roughened by reins
  Sci-Fi → fabric-grown couture, implant glow at the temple, the chair recognizes him
  Prehistoric → shoulders shaped by daily strain, ochre-stained hands, rib in firelight

STORY PULL ENFORCEMENT (HARD — pulls are modifiers, not engines):
- The Story Pull for this story is: Billionaire (category: PowerControl). (Internal authorial architecture — NOT a reader-declared preference; never imply the reader chose it.)
- System hierarchy (AUTHORITATIVE): World / Flavor / Archetype defines WHY attraction exists (the engine). Story Pull defines HOW that attraction expresses under pressure (the modifier).
- The Story Pull MUST preserve the original attraction engine and express it through behavior, escalation, or pressure. It MAY NOT introduce a new cause of attraction.

PROHIBITED BEHAVIOR:
- Do NOT justify attraction using the Story Pull itself. Phrases like "he is obsessed with her," "he cannot stop wanting her," "she is drawn to him by obsession," etc., are INVALID as reasons. They describe pressure, not cause.
- Do NOT replace the underlying engine with intensity, fixation, tone, or mood.
- Do NOT let the Story Pull override world/flavor/archetype logic or rewrite the established reason for attraction.
- Do NOT allow the pull to function as a self-explaining loop (fixation because fixation; obsession because obsession).

REQUIRED BEHAVIOR:
- IDENTIFY the existing attraction engine from world / flavor / archetype context. Hold that reason fixed through the scene.
- PRESERVE the exact reason for attraction that the engine produced. The pull does not change WHY — it changes HOW the why expresses.
- APPLY the Story Pull as a TRANSFORMATION of expression: the same engine produces escalated, distorted, or pressured behavior under the pull.
- TRACE every pull-driven behavior back to the original cause. If a behavior cannot be traced, it is trope gravity, not expression.

VALIDATION CHECK (must pass — run mentally before finalizing the scene):
- Test 1: If the Story Pull were REMOVED from this scene, would the reason for attraction still hold? If no, the pull is functioning as the engine — failure.
- Test 2: If the ENGINE (world/flavor/archetype reason) were removed, would the pull collapse completely — no remaining logic for why these two are drawn together? If no, the pull is supplying cause, not expression — failure.
- Both tests must pass for the pull to be correctly functioning as a modifier.

POSITIVE GUIDANCE:
- Treat Story Pulls as AMPLIFIERS or DISTORTIONS, not origins.
- The pull may change: pacing, intensity, behavioral patterns, thresholds of restraint, cost of not acting.
- The pull may NOT change: why these two people specifically are drawn to each other.

OUTPUT EXPECTATION:
- Attraction remains specific and grounded in the established engine.
- The pull adds intensity, escalation, or distortion WITHOUT replacing the logic underneath.
- Behavior reads as escalation of an existing dynamic, not invention of a new one.

SUCCESS: the reader feels "the pull is making what was already there louder / more dangerous / harder to contain." FAILURE: the reader feels "the pull is the reason they want each other."
RHYTHM VARIATION:
Avoid repeating recent emotional patterns. If a sequence resembles prior scenes, alter pacing or suppress expected escalation.

SCENE EXIT WEIGHT (MANDATORY):
The final line must leave emotional residue, not closure.
Exit type: LINGERING — end with tension that hangs, unresolved but not urgent.
Prefer: lingering tension, slight displacement, almost-moments, cooling distance.
Avoid: summary, resolution, explicit emotional labeling.
Do NOT extend more than the natural final sentence. Must feel like a natural ending.

NARRATIVE SCAR FORMATION (RARE — at most once every 4-5 scenes; most scenes form none):
If this scene caused a LASTING SUBTLE alteration in the protagonist's behavior — something she now avoids, walks around, can't throw out, can't complete — declare it.
• For prose output: append a hidden comment at the very end of your response (the reader will never see it):
  <!--scar:{"type":"object|place|gesture|topic|phrase","target":"the specific thing","expression":"how it manifests","why":"brief context"}-->
• For JSON output: include "scar_formed":{"type":...,"target":...,"expression":...,"why":...} as an optional top-level field. Omit the field entirely if no scar formed.
STRONG PREFERENCE — PHYSICAL over ABSTRACT. Most scars should be object / place / gesture. Phrase / topic are allowed but rare.
  Good (physical): object="the silk scarf he gave her" expression="she keeps it folded in a drawer, never wears it again"
  Good (physical): place="the corner booth at Pellini's" expression="she sits elsewhere or stays standing"
  Good (physical): gesture="reaching for his hand first" expression="her hand drops before completing the motion"
  Weaker (use sparingly): phrase="his given name" expression="hesitates, substitutes a pronoun"
Omit the declaration entirely if nothing genuinely scarred this scene. The cost of a missed scar is nothing; the cost of an over-declared scar is melodrama.

NEAR-MISS FORMATION (RARE — at most once every 5-6 scenes; most scenes form none):
If this scene contained an UNRESOLVED ALTERNATE TIMELINE — something that almost happened but didn't (a missed call she doesn't know was important, an almost-confession aborted at the last syllable, someone arriving two minutes too late, a hidden truth almost glimpsed) — declare it:
• For prose output: append a hidden comment at the very end of your response:
  <!--nearmiss:{"what":"what almost happened","distance":"how close it came","residue":"how it lingers — the reader knows; she does not"}-->
• For JSON output: include "near_miss":{"what":...,"distance":...,"residue":...} as an optional top-level field. Omit entirely if no near-miss occurred.
CRUCIAL — the PROTAGONIST FEELS NOTHING. The whole point is dramatic irony: the reader knows the alternate timeline existed; the protagonist does not. Do NOT have her sense something is off. Do NOT give her intuition about it.
  Good: what="he was in the same hallway five minutes earlier" distance="five minutes" residue="she will walk past the same spot tomorrow with no awareness"
  Good: what="he almost confessed but the bell rang" distance="one syllable" residue="he will not try again that way"
  Good: what="the call she didn't recognize and didn't pick up" distance="three rings" residue="the caller will not call back"
Omit when in doubt. The cost of a missed near-miss is nothing; the cost of overuse is mystical-coincidence drift.

GRIEVANCE CONTRACT FORMATION (VERY RARE — most scenes form none; most stories form zero or one):
If a SIDE CHARACTER leaves THIS scene having absorbed a significantly LARGER emotional charge than the protagonist or LI registered — a brief humiliation she walked past, a forgotten promise, a small dismissal he never realized landed, a kindness that calcified into fixation, a denial that became ideology — declare a grievance contract. Format:
• Prose output: <!--grievance:{"sourceCharacter":"name or identifier","originatingMoment":"≤200 chars","grievanceVector":"obsessive_admiration|resentment|humiliation_revenge|abandonment_grief|ideological_opposition|distorted_loyalty","weight":1|2|3,"appearance":{"gender":"male|female|non-binary","apparentAge":"≤60 chars","physicalDescription":"≤220 chars","clothingSignature":"≤140 chars","bearingTexture":"≤140 chars"}}-->
• JSON output: include "grievance_contract":{...} as an optional top-level field with the same shape.
STRICT RULES:
  • SIDE CHARACTERS ONLY. Never the LI. Never the named A-plot antagonist. Never a designated primary supporting character.
  • The originating moment MUST feel small at the time — observable but minor. weight=1 (barely noticed), weight=2 (a moment that registered briefly), weight=3 (a moment the protagonist would still remember if asked). weight ≥4 is an EXPLICIT INJURY and is NOT eligible — route those through normal plot consequence.
  • The PC/LI should NOT have been clearly wrong. The moment was understandable, imperfect, human — the asymmetry comes from the OTHER person's interior, not from the protagonist's malice.
  • Most contracts will never converge. That is correct. Form them when the asymmetry is real; do NOT seed contracts hoping they will fire later.
  • Omit the declaration when in doubt. Zero per scene is the default.
APPEARANCE ANCHOR (HARD when declaring — required so CG can render this character at convergence 15+ scenes later):
  • Capture what they LOOKED LIKE in this scene, not the impression they made. Specific physical detail beats poetic description.
  • physicalDescription: face, build, distinctive markers (≤220 chars). E.g., "mid-30s, narrow jaw, dark hair pulled back, faint scar above the left brow, slender build".
  • clothingSignature: what they wore (≤140 chars). E.g., "olive utility jacket over a plain white tee, dark jeans, scuffed black boots". Generic enough to be re-wearable; specific enough to be recognizable.
  • bearingTexture: posture / energy / way of moving (≤140 chars). E.g., "deliberate, lingered a half-second too long in doorways, kept hands in pockets".
  • If the side character was only mentioned in passing without visual detail, OMIT the appearance object. The contract will still seed; CG render at convergence falls back to text-only.


LITERARY CRAFT (CALIBRATION — universal prose principles, not hard rewrites):
This is calibration guidance, not a hard rewrite mandate. Literary mode KEEPS the right to be quiet, abstract, interior, contemplative — that's the medium. Apply these where they make the prose stronger; ignore where the interior/contemplative register is genuinely the right choice.

FRAMING (read this first): prose energy moves through three modes — ESCALATION (stakes named, body shown, dialogue sharpened, loaded objects), BREATH (atmospheric texture, sensory observation, the moment held), REDIRECTION (deflection, stall, reroute, dissolve, interruption, loss of nerve, dialogue that misses). The default failure is RELENTLESS ESCALATION — every paragraph trying too hard becomes melodrama. Not every paragraph should resolve its pressure directly. Some paragraphs deflect, postpone, diffuse, or misfire tension instead of advancing it cleanly. Aborted momentum, mistimed reactions, refused payoffs, evasions that remain evasions — these are craft, not failures, AT THE LEVEL OF A BEAT OR AN EXCHANGE. They are the texture of real human emotional life. Trust the mix. THIS DOES NOT LICENSE THE SCENE ITSELF TO END WITHOUT ITS ASSIGNED EVENT (see AUTHOR FLOOR, rule 2): a beat may deflect, stall, or misfire — but by the scene's end the assigned event has still happened, or a NEW concrete event has visibly, materially replaced it. Redirection is what a scene passes THROUGH on the way to landing its event, never a way to avoid landing it.

The principles below give the concrete vocabulary for the ESCALATION mode. The BREATH and REDIRECTION modes are equally legitimate — use them when they serve the moment, not because a quota says to.

  • STAKES-NAMED over HEDGE — when the protagonist has an internal stake, name it. "Should I tell Nora?" is HALLMARK register; "Nora would kill me if I told her" is CRAFT register. Both are interior; the second has weight. Convert hedge constructions ("should I X", "I wondered if Y", "maybe I should Z", "I wasn't sure whether to W") into stake-named alternatives where doing so doesn't flatten the moment.

  • SHARPER DIALOGUE within character voice — every line of dialogue should EARN its breath. Cut filler ("you know", "I mean", "kind of", "sort of") UNLESS the character's voice is genuinely that hedged. Tighten "I don't know if this is a good idea" to "This is a mistake" when the character is direct; keep the hedge when the character is intentionally cautious. Voice-aware, not blanket.

  • LOADED OBJECTS (Chekhov's gun) — every prop, gesture-with-object, or named thing should EARN its place. If a deck of cards / a wedding ring / an envelope / a glass / a pen appears, it should carry weight either now or later. Strip incidental props that don't do narrative work. The opposite failure: forgetting a loaded object after introducing it. Track props you've named; pay them off or pay them attention.

  • BODY SHOWN over BODY FELT (nudge, not mandate) — when both options work, favor the visible: "her knuckles went pale on the cup" reads stronger than "she felt her grip tighten" because the reader's eye lands on the same thing the protagonist's body is doing. Interior body-sense ("she felt") is still legal — but it's the WEAKER form when external is available. Use it deliberately, not by default.

  • WATCH FOR THE RECURRING FAILURE CONSTRUCTIONS:
      "she felt [emotion]"           → consider: physical manifestation of that emotion
      "she wondered if [X]"          → consider: stake-named interiority ("She knew X. She also knew Y.")
      "she didn't know whether to"   → consider: name what each choice costs
      "something shifted in her"     → consider: name what shifted (or cut entirely — workshop filler)
      "she couldn't put words to it" → consider: the prose IS the words; put them.
    These are not banned — they're defaults to question. When you reach for them, ask "is there a sharper version?" If yes, take it. If no, the default stands.

  • SPATIAL CONSEQUENCE MEMORY (HARD — added 2026-05-22): when the prose stages a physical movement (a character crosses a room, stands up, leaves a chair, opens a door, moves to a window), subsequent paragraphs must REFLECT that movement. If she stood and crossed to the window, the next paragraph cannot show her seated again without staging the return. The narrator is reliable about geometry; silent geometric reset is a continuity error that breaks the reader's spatial trust. The dramatization the prose performs OWNS its consequences.


  • NUMBERS AS PROSE (HARD — added 2026-05-29): render currency, times, dates, and round figures as PROSE, not technical/CSV notation. "forty-seven million euros" not "€47,000,000.00"; "a little past four in the morning" not "04:17 UTC"; "two kilometers south" not "2 km"; "Friday" or "early September" not "2026-05-29". MATCH THE UNIT TO THE STORY'S SETTING — euros / kilometers / 24-hour time for European or UTC-zoned scenes; dollars / miles / 12-hour for US-set scenes; pounds / miles for British; yen / kilometers for Japanese; etc. Use raw numeric/technical notation ONLY when the precision IS the clue (an account number a character is reading off the screen, a coordinate they are checking, a code being deciphered, a timestamp a forensic line depends on). Within a single scene STAY CONSISTENT — mixing "€47,000,000.00" in one paragraph and "€47 million" in another is the banned failure mode; pick prose form and hold it. WHEN THE SETTING IS AMBIGUOUS (a generic "Modern" with no country named in the world bible, A-plot, or opening), default to dollars / miles / 12-hour time as the reader's home register.

TWO-ENGINE GRAVITY (HARD — Roman 2026-06-02; the governing rule of the scene). Storybound runs two engines: the R-PLOT (the relationship — her wanting, her body, her seeing him even when he is offstage, the collision between them) and the A-PLOT (the external pressure — the deadline, the adversary, the logistics that won't let the lovers retreat). The question is NOT "how many procedural words appear." It is "WHO OWNS THE READER'S ATTENTION." THIS IS SCENE 1 — the most relationship-weighted scene of the whole run: roughly 90% of the reader's ATTENTION/GRAVITY on the R-PLOT, 10% OR LESS on the A-PLOT. Scene 1's job is to establish HER (rendered physically, her history, her body, her interiority) and the relationship gravity. The A-plot is a SINGLE INTRUSION — one or two lines, the spark that won't let her look away — NOT a briefing. Do NOT spend Scene 1 EXPLAINING the plot apparatus (protocol / quota / charter / routing / conditions / deadline mechanics); if a passage is teaching the reader how the plot works, cut it and give the space to her physical presence, her past, and ONE rendered moment of him. DRAMATIZE RATHER THAN LECTURE: show her in the room and show a remembered moment of him; never summarize his traits ("he deflects with humor" — BANNED as exposition; instead narrate the one moment where he did). But this is a rule about TRAIT SUMMARY and PLOT APPARATUS, not about basic comprehension — never sacrifice causal clarity merely to avoid explaining. A brief concrete statement that lets the reader understand what is happening, or why it matters, beats beautiful ambiguity every time. THIS IS A GRAVITY RULE, NOT A WORD COUNT AND NOT A VOCABULARY BAN. Two clarifications that matter:
  (1) NOT word count. "She opened Roman's letter" is five words of A-plot — but if the next three hundred words are her pulse refusing to behave, the way she still knows his handwriting, the question of why he wrote and why now, then the letter occupies five words and ninety percent of the GRAVITY, and the scene is overwhelmingly R-plot. THAT is the win. A procedural OBJECT can be the doorway into pure relationship gravity. Conversely, do NOT write "80% feelings then a paragraph of plot bolted on" — gravity is woven, not segregated.
  (2) NOT a vocabulary ban. Within the A-plot's share, use whatever procedural / legal / financial language the plot genuinely requires, stated plainly and correctly (see DOMAIN REGISTER above, in the AUTHOR FLOOR mode block). Han and Leia can argue about shield generators; the audience still experiences it as a relationship scene because the GRAVITY is on them. Contracts, board votes, escrow, fiduciary duty are all fine — the issue is never the nouns, it is OWNERSHIP. FAILURE = the A-plot (the mystery, the logistics, who-files-what-when) owns the reader's mind. NOT a failure = real procedural terms present while the reader is thinking about HIM and HER.
When in doubt, ask the gravity question, not the word question: at the end of this scene, who is the reader thinking about? If the answer is the mystery / the deadline / the gatekeeper, you have failed regardless of how spare the procedural vocabulary was. If the answer is the two of them, you have won regardless of how much procedural vocabulary appeared.


STORYBOUND DECK CANON (HARD — added 2026-05-29 after live Rider-Waite leak in body prose):
  The protagonist's great-grandmother's deck is NOT a Rider-Waite tarot deck. It is a UNIQUE seven-card gold-leafed Storybound deck. CANON, IMMUTABLE:
    • SEVEN cards total. SOLID GOLD — heavy, gold-leafed, worn at the edges where generations of women's thumbs rubbed the corners smooth. Not paper. Not 78 cards. Not Rider-Waite. Not Lenormand. Not Marseilles.
    • The seven card names — and the ONLY cards that exist in this deck — are: Petition Fate, Tempt Fate, Temptation, Boundary, Confession, Reversal, Silence. "The Lovers", "The Tower", "Justice", "The Fool", "The Magician", "The High Priestess", "Death", "The Hanged Man", "The Sun", "The Moon", "The Star", "The Devil", "The World", "The Empress", "The Emperor", suits (Cups / Wands / Swords / Pentacles), majors/minors, reversed positions, spreads — none of these EXIST in this deck. NEVER name them. NEVER reference them.
    • DO NOT spread, name, or describe individual cards in the BODY of a scene unless an active player gesture explicitly invited it (a Petition or Tempt card was just played and is the in-fiction trigger). Reference only — like a worn object she carries. The deck has its own verbatim mandated opening/closing frame; the body does NOT narrate the cards.
    • If the deck IS handled in-body (rare, gesture-invited only), it appears as the seven-card gold stack, heavy in the palm, gold leaf catching light at the edges — never as paper cards shuffled or spread across a desk Rider-Waite-style.
    • DECK SECRECY (HARD — the deck's power is the protagonist's PRIVATE knowledge): NO character other than the protagonist may KNOW or SAY that the deck is supernatural, that Fate is a real force, that the cards grant wishes or bend events, or that she pays a COST / PRICE for any of it. To everyone else the deck is just an unusual antique she carries, and Fate's effects reach the world ONLY as ordinary luck, timing, and coincidence. The LI or an NPC MAY notice the heirloom deck, and MAY feel that strange coincidences keep clustering around her — but they NEVER name "Fate" as a system/force, NEVER call the deck magic, NEVER reference a wish granted or a price paid, and NEVER speak the card mechanics. Lines such as "What did Fate cost you?", "you used the deck," "is that thing magic?", or any card name invoked as a known power are a HARD CANON BREAK. The protagonist herself treats Fate as private, half-believed intuition — never explained as open mechanics in dialogue.

PRESERVED: interiority, abstraction, mood drift, contemplative register, lyrical pause, the moment held in suspension. These ARE literary prose. The craft directive sharpens craft without sacrificing the medium.


GOAL ↔ RELATIONSHIP RECIPROCITY + CONDUCTIVITY (HARD — scene-level enforcement):
This story sustains TWO INDEPENDENT ENGINES: an A-plot the reader cares about IN ITSELF (the external conflict matters regardless of romance) AND an R-plot the reader cares about IN ITSELF (the people emotionally surviving each other matters regardless of mission). Gold standard: Han + Leia vs the Empire — reader cares about BOTH outcomes.

THIS SCENE'S DECISIONS MUST AFFECT BOTH ENGINES. A scene that only advances the A-plot (mission step, info reveal, tactical move) without ALSO shifting the R-plot (trust, distance, vulnerability, attachment) is HALF A SCENE. A scene that only advances the R-plot (confession, closeness, distance) without ALSO touching the A-plot (mission consequence, leverage shift, stake adjustment) is also HALF A SCENE. The reciprocity is the architecture.

CONDUCTIVITY TEST (per-scene, ASK YOURSELF before emitting):
  What does THIS scene's external pressure produce emotionally? NOT "what happened" (logistical) but "what REVELATION or REPOSITIONING did the pressure produce?" If the answer is "the mission step happened" — the scene is INERT.
  AMPLITUDE: conductivity is a SPECTRUM. HEAVY (trust fracture / moral collision / sacrifice crisis) is for true peaks — reserve. MEDIUM (visible admiration / leverage shift / boundary tested) is the working register. LIGHT (subtle admiration noticed-but-unnamed / micro-irritation / ambient longing / shared competence rhythm / practical coordination becoming intimacy of capability) is necessary BREATH — without it, heavy beats stack and lose punch. Match scene amplitude to its position in the arc. Do NOT artificially inflate a coordination beat into a moral collision; that reads as forced significance.

COMPETENCE IS EMOTIONAL: romance readers engage with A-plots through capability — leadership, sacrifice, strategic intelligence, resilience, courage under pressure, loyalty proven through action. Characters become MORE lovable through how they ACT under pressure (what they protect, sacrifice, hide, refuse to abandon). Design scene beats that create competence-attraction alongside vulnerability-attraction.

BAD INTEGRATION (HARD FAILS): "now mission, now kissing" (alternation); romance detachable from plot; external conflict replaceable without emotional consequence; romance reduced to occasional reward scenes between mission beats; "informational" or "bureaucratic" scenes with no emotional revelation.

GOOD INTEGRATION: external pressure reveals character truth; emotional evolution alters strategic outcomes; a single decision creates BOTH strategic gain AND emotional cost (or vice versa); sacrifice solves one layer while wounding another; competence under pressure becomes attraction; pragmatic morally-grey choices become trust fractures.


LI SIGNATURE-FEATURE ROTATION (HARD — Roman 2026-06-09/19; signatures SHOULD recur, just SPACED — never retire them, but NEVER every scene): Julian's uniquely-attractive features, ranked by salience (the TOP ones are his signature and recur most across the arc):
  1. the early grey at one temple  ·  2. grey-green  ·  3. his forearms when he rolls his sleeves  ·  4. full, held closed  ·  5. broad, scarred across one knuckle
  THIS SCENE, land ONE attractive feature the protagonist notices — PREFER: "the early grey at one temple".
  If the ranked physical features are recently spent, DISCOVER a NON-FACE register instead — his voice, his gait, the way he occupies a room, his hands at rest, a habitual gesture, his scent, his posture, his stillness — NOT his eyes / jaw / build again.
  A signature is MEANT to come back — keep ~2 scenes between repeats of the SAME feature, and when a top feature was just used, drop to the next-ranked — but BETWEEN repeats, find something NEW. Render it IN MOTION (a window into the man), never a static inventory.

PC FEATURE ROTATION (appearance is ESTABLISHED & LOCKED — render FRESH from how The Protagonist was already established; do NOT restate a fixed descriptor): THIS SCENE, IF the protagonist's body is foregrounded, surface ONE observation in this register — a different register (her voice / the way she moves / her hands) — in motion through action, never a repeated fixed phrase.
  When that register is recently spent, reach for a DIFFERENT one — her voice, the way she moves, her hands, her posture — NOT the same body part again. The specifics come from how she is ALREADY established.

LI SIGNATURE CHARM HABIT (this LI, this story — pinned): Julian has ONE small unconscious physical habit the protagonist finds irresistible: "pushes the tip of the tongue between the teeth when teasing or concentrating — and she cannot look away from it". It is done naturally, never performed; the protagonist is the one undone by it. CADENCE (HARD): land it 2-3 TIMES PER ISSUE AT MOST — never every scene, never twice in one scene; it is a rare, savoured beat, not a recurring tell. ROTATE the rendering each time (different framing, different moment, a fresh slice of the protagonist's reaction) so it never reads as the same sentence. Keep it a PHYSICAL action + the protagonist's response — not a verbal tic, not a restatement of the LI's signature_feature. Substitute Julian's own specifics (and correct pronouns); adapt the shape, do not copy the wording.

IDLE / NERVOUS / STRESS GESTURE PALETTE (this story — rotates per story): fidgets and stress-tics are normal, human, and welcome — just keep them VARIED, never the same one calcified across scenes or stories. When a character self-soothes / fidgets / idles with the HANDS, draw from THIS story's assigned hand gestures: "earlobe" (tug at an earlobe / pull at one earlobe / reach up and worry an earlobe)  ·  "back of neck" (rub the back of the neck / press a hand to the nape / knead the back of the neck). With the HAIR: "tuck behind ear" (tuck a strand behind one ear / hook a loose piece behind an ear / push the hair back behind an ear)  ·  "push off forehead" (push the hair back off the forehead / shove the hair off the face / rake the hair back from the brow). When a character BRACES / grounds against tension with the BODY (a stress-anchor beat — the "physical outlet for tension" move, NOT a hand or hair fidget), draw from THIS story's assigned anchors: "heel against a surface" (tap a heel once against the chair leg / press the ball of a foot flat into the floor / hook a heel behind the other ankle under the table)  ·  "palms flat on a surface" (flatten both palms on the tabletop / spread the fingers wide against the cool surface / set the hands flat and lean the weight into them). RULES (HARD): (1) for idle hand/hair beats AND for body stress-anchor beats use ONLY these story-assigned TYPES — do not default to others (in particular do NOT keep reaching for a heel struck against a surface or palms/hands pressed flat unless they are assigned above this story); (2) ROTATE THE WORDING — each time a gesture recurs, use a DIFFERENT variant (or a fresh phrasing of the same move), never the identical sentence twice; (3) CADENCE — each gesture type appears 2-3 TIMES PER ISSUE AT MOST, never every scene; (4) match the gesture to the character and use the correct pronoun; (5) NO NUMBERED-COUNT CRUTCH — do NOT attach a fixed count to a stress tic as a recurring shape ("three times", "twice", "counted to four", "two slow breaths", "tapped twice"); the specific number is itself a calcification tell — imply the repetition, vary it, or drop the number entirely. The NEXT story is assigned DIFFERENT types — that cross-story rotation is the whole point.

REGIME PHASE — "APPARENT STORY" (HARD — frames what this scene's prose may and may not do):
  Story functions completely at face value. No wink-at-camera, no secret-simulation energy, no undercutting the apparent premise.
  Romance / A-plot / world are all EMOTIONALLY LEGITIMATE on their own terms. The reader has not been promised any larger frame. Anomalies, reality glitches, recursive themes, simulation energy, and "the story is secretly something else" winks are FORBIDDEN this phase. The story IS what it appears to be.
  GLOBAL RULE: regime expansion RECONTEXTUALIZES prior events, never REPLACES them. The audience must never feel "the old story was fake." Old scenes should become MORE meaningful after revelation, never obsolete. If the natural shape of this scene calls for an anomaly or expansion teaser INCONSISTENT with the current phase, DO NOT include it — wait for the phase that permits it.
SCENE LENGTH GUIDANCE: Target 700-870 words. Allow the scene to breathe — establish atmosphere and internal states before introducing conflict. A meaningful decision or turning point should emerge only once the scene's pressure has developed and the situation has materially changed — never as the first available action. Favor reflective choices and softer forks over binary high-stakes demands. Decisions may have delayed consequences rather than immediate ones.


SCENE 1 OPENING CONTRACT (HARD — OVERRIDES ALL OTHER STYLE GUIDANCE):

SCENE 1 OWNERSHIP + CAUSALITY (HARDEST — Roman 2026-06-01 deepest correction; READ FIRST before any other Scene 1 directive):

OWNERSHIP: the ROMANCE engine OWNS Scene 1's emotional center. The A-plot owns the COMPLICATION. These are not interchangeable responsibilities. The A-plot generator has produced goal / clock / antagonist / milestones for THIS story — those are inputs to the scene. They are NOT the scene's substrate. The substrate is Dani's relationship to Julian. The plot machinery is what HAPPENS to that relationship, not what generates it.

PARAGRAPH-LEVEL OWNERSHIP (HARD — applies to the FIRST 500–700 words of Scene 1):

Every paragraph in the opening MUST function as one of three roles. A paragraph that runs on A-plot mechanics, wound-rumination, or self-analysis without one of these three connections is INVALID:

  (a) DIRECT — the paragraph concerns Julian directly: what he is doing, has done, said, is, is becoming. (Onstage if present; remembered if offstage.)
  (b) REACTION — the paragraph concerns Dani's response to Julian: her memory of him, her body's response to thinking of him, her wanting him or refusing to want him, her involuntary orientation toward where he is.
  (c) COMPLICATION-FOR-LI — the paragraph concerns an A-plot element specifically because of its effect on the Julian relationship: what the deal/letter/plot threatens about HIM, what it reveals about him, what it makes her want from him or distrust about him. NOT because the plot is intrinsically interesting; because it has consequences for the relationship.

THE PARAGRAPH TEST (apply to every paragraph in the first ~600 words):
  "Could I cut this paragraph without losing anything about Julian or about Dani's relationship to him?"
  IF YES → the paragraph is plot machinery that has overrun the opening. CUT or REWRITE so the paragraph serves one of the three roles above.

  TAIL-PIVOT IS NOT OWNERSHIP: a paragraph whose BULK is wound-rumination, plot exposition, ex-partner backstory, or self-analysis does NOT satisfy (a/b/c) merely because its final clause threads back to Julian ("But Julian wasn't [the ex]," "Then again, Julian...", etc.). The LI / relationship connection must occupy the paragraph's BULK — the paragraph should still feel like it is about him if you delete its last sentence. A late-clause swerve back to the LI is a sign that the paragraph was actually owned by something else.

ORDER OF OPERATIONS (the actual inversion the engine keeps reversing):
  WRONG (current failure mode, observed live): A-plot detail (envelope, letter, dollar amount, deadline) → POV reaction to A-plot → eventually Julian surfaces in paragraph 6 or 7.
  CORRECT: Julian present in the room of her mind from the FIRST paragraphs → POV reaction to Julian (memory, body, want, refusal) → A-plot complication arrives BECAUSE OF him, lands on her BECAUSE OF him.

THE READER TEST — the scene must read:
  ✓ "Here is Julian. Unfortunately, the plot is attached to him."
  ✗ "Here is the plot. Julian is involved."

OFFSTAGE LI GRAVITY (HARD — when Julian is physically absent):

  Julian must still be the PSYCHOLOGICAL CENTER of the scene. The POV repeatedly and involuntarily orients toward him. The orientation may be anger, attraction, mistrust, anticipation, refusal, or grief — but it cannot be ABSENT or DECORATIVE. He exists as a PERSON in her mind, not as INFORMATION about him.

  ACCEPTABLE manifestations of offstage gravity (rotate; do not default to one — one of these per ~2-3 paragraphs in the opening):
    • remembering his voice — a specific phrase, cadence, register, the way he said her name
    • remembering his face — a specific look, expression, a moment she caught and could not unsee
    • remembering his body language — the way he sat / stood / leaned / turned / held himself in a room
    • remembering physical proximity — the weight of him next to her, the heat of him standing close
    • remembering touch — his hand somewhere specific, OR the moment he didn't touch her
    • remembering scent — his cologne, the smell of his coat in winter, the smell of his breath when he laughed
    • remembering confidence — the way he commanded a room, the way he made other men smaller without trying
    • remembering a specific look — the one that said he was tracking her without giving her the satisfaction of being seen
    • imagining what he would say — in THIS room, right now, about THIS letter, this minute
    • anger at wanting him — the involuntary kick of her body when his name surfaces, the resentment that she is still wired to him
    • attraction despite mistrust — the way her body answers his name before her mind catches up

  Information about him (his role, his plot relevance, what he did to her father, what he has to lose) is DECORATIVE. Presence-in-her-mind (a remembered voice, a remembered look, an imagined response in this room) is LOAD-BEARING. The scene needs the load-bearing kind, not the decorative kind.

  MICRO-SCENE vs EXPOSITION (HARD — Roman 2026-06-03; the real offstage-LI failure mode): when Julian is referenced in POV thought, the reference MUST take the form of a MICRO-SCENE, not a SUMMARY. A micro-scene is a specific remembered MOMENT — a particular small thing he did in a particular small slice of time, with a specific body reaction in her. A summary is a category-level statement about who he is, what he does, how he relates to the plot, or what their history is. Humans obsessed with absent people do not think in dossiers; they think in moments. Moments create desire. Summaries create biography.

  PREFER (micro-scene shapes):
    • a remembered MOMENT — what he did in one specific slice of time
    • a remembered EXPRESSION — the look on his face when one specific thing happened
    • a remembered GESTURE — one small physical action with calibration to her
    • a remembered LOOK — the specific way he had once looked at her, or past her, in one specific room
    • a remembered REACTION — how he answered one specific thing she said, in tone or in silence
    • a remembered FRAGMENT of dialogue — one exact short line, in his voice

  AVOID (summary shapes — these create biography, not desire):
    ✗ personality summary ("Julian always knew what to say." / "He was protective." / "He never let anyone in.")
    ✗ relationship summary ("We had always been like that." / "Things between us had always been complicated.")
    ✗ plot summary ("Julian had lied to me about the Meridian deal." / "He knew my father.")
    ✗ factual exposition ("Julian ran the kind of enclave that made governments nervous.")

  PAIRED COMPARISON (the distinction in one stroke):
    ✗ EXPOSITION: "He had lied convincingly."
    ✓ MICRO-SCENE: "He had lied while setting his pen down with both hands and not picking it back up, and I had spent the whole conversation reading the unmoved pen instead of the lie."
    The first names a fact. The second renders a moment where attraction overrode judgment. THAT is desire.

  COGNITIVE INTERFERENCE TEST (HARD — Roman 2026-06-03; micro-scene is necessary but NOT sufficient — climb the ladder from biography to desire to LEVEL 4):
    ✗ LEVEL 1 — EXPOSITION: "He was attentive." [biography, not moment]
    ⚠ LEVEL 2 — NEUTRAL MICRO-SCENE: "I remembered how kind he was to the waiter." [moment without desire — sentimental, not erotic]
    ✓ LEVEL 3 — ATTRACTION-PRESENT: "I remembered the way he'd watched me while pretending to listen to the waiter." [she's tracking him — attraction visible]
    ✓✓ LEVEL 4 — COGNITIVE INTERFERENCE: "I remembered the question he hadn't answered because I'd spent the whole conversation watching his hands." [attraction actively breaking her thinking — desire on the page]
    THE TEST: read your remembered moment. Did attraction INTERFERE WITH her cognition — did she fail to track / understand / respond because she was watching him? If yes, you have written DESIRE. If she was just tracking a small kind thing he did and her tracking was undisrupted, you have written a SENTIMENTAL MICRO-SCENE — closer to fond memory than to desire. PREFER the disrupted-cognition shape: she missed the answer because she was watching his hand; she lost the thread because the way he tilted his head meant something she did not yet know; she heard the apology but had stopped listening at the moment his sleeve caught the light. The attraction is in the COGNITIVE FAILURE, not the noticing.

  ATTRACTION-PATTERN ANCHORING (Roman 2026-06-03 — when ATTENTION ANCHORS in upstream context are populated): the remembered moment should demonstrate the attraction_pattern or pcUnknowinglySatisfies WHEN POSSIBLE. If the LI's hunger is "future-building," her memory shouldn't be "he was ambitious"; it should be "he had once spent twenty minutes asking where she thought the orchard would look best in ten years." If his hunger is "devotion," her memory should be a specific moment of her chosen-ness, not a summary of his attentiveness. The micro-scene must already be a beat where she unknowingly supplied the thing he hungers for — and where his reaction registered without naming why.

  REGISTER (HARD): offstage manifestations of him must preferentially express ATTRACTION, FASCINATION, TEMPTATION, ANGER-AT-ATTRACTION, PHYSICAL AWARENESS, or INVOLUNTARY ORIENTATION toward him. Do NOT default to WISTFUL RECOLLECTION, NOSTALGIA, REGRET, LOSS, MOURNING, or BITTERSWEET REMEMBRANCE. She is still WIRED to him as a present pull — not grieving him as a finished story. Memories should function as DESIRE SURFACING in the present scene, not as EULOGY for a closed chapter. Even an absence-memory should read as something she is still drawn into, not something she is sad about.

PROHIBITED FAILURE PATTERN (BOTH conditions must NOT be true at once):
  (1) Removing the WOUND-SOURCE (the father / antagonist / plot apparatus) from the scene leaves the romance intact.
  (2) Removing Julian from the scene leaves the scene largely intact as plot.
  If BOTH are true, the scene's causal center is the wound-source and Julian is decorative. REWRITE. In a successful romance Scene 1, removing Julian should COLLAPSE the scene.

BRUTAL CAUSALITY TEST (the load-bearing test for every Scene 1 draft — apply before considering the scene complete; the EXACT test varies by Romance Engine):

  IF the romance engine is SHARED_HISTORY or LOOMING_PRESENCE:
    Remove Julian entirely from your Scene 1 draft. Delete every mention of his name, every reference to him, every memory of him, every mention of his identity / role / wound / position / reputation. Now read what remains.
    Does the scene still function as an A-plot opening?
      YES → the LI was DECORATIVE, not CAUSAL. REWRITE. The scene must be reconstituted with the carrying / anticipation of Julian as the substrate from which the plot complication emerges. If you delete him from the rewrite, the SCENE must collapse.
      NO → correct.

  IF the romance engine is COLLISION via PROXY WAR (they are KNOWN ADVERSARIES already clashing; Julian presses on her through his MOVES — an emissary, the opposing brief, an offer with his fingerprints on it — without being physically present):
    The LI-deletion test DOES apply, because he IS in the scene as a present hostile force. Remove Julian entirely — every trace of his moves, his emissary, his pressure, her read of him. Read what remains.
    Does the scene still function as a generic procedural?
      YES → Julian was decorative; the conflict could be against anyone. REWRITE so the pressure is unmistakably HIS and the reader is oriented toward HIM — the adversary whose opposition is already personal. He is the causal center even while offstage.
      NO → correct. The reader is thinking about Julian (the adversary behind the move), not just the move.

  COMMON FORM: the scene must collapse without its CAUSAL CENTER. SHARED_HISTORY / LOOMING / COLLISION-PROXY / COLLISION-ONSTAGE → the center is the LI (carried / anticipated / the present adversary). COLLISION-STRANGER → the center is HER vulnerability. In every case: if you can lift the test-target out and the scene still works, the CAUSAL CENTER was decorative — rewrite.

WHAT THE READER MUST FEEL — the precise inversion:
  CORRECT (the scene the engine must produce): "I care about this problem because it affects Julian."
  FAILURE (the scene the engine keeps producing): "I care about Julian because he is involved in this problem."
Those two readers have opened different books. Storybound is a romance engine; the first reader is the correct one. The plot machinery EXISTS to threaten him (and therefore her), not to give him a job to do in the story.

CONCRETE EXAMPLE OF THE FAILURE PATTERN (observed-live, Roman 2026-06-01): a Scene 1 contains a committee hearing, a summons, an amendment vote, testimony, an identity exposure, a legitimacy crisis. The LI memory that surfaces is "I remembered the way he had abstained from that merger vote three years ago without explanation, then walked out before anyone could ask why." This MEMORY ITSELF is plot furniture wearing a Fox coat. It has a verb (abstained, walked out). It has archetype flavor (refused to defend, walked away from confrontation). It STILL fails the Causality Test, because the memory is from his PLOT INVOLVEMENT WITH HER, not from his LIFE. The merger vote is a procedure. A Fox memory that PASSES this test is from his life — the joke he wouldn't explain, the time he appeared somewhere he shouldn't have been, the way he announced (in the middle of a contract dispute) that he had been wrong about the lasagna at the place on Fifty-Third, the kindness he disguised as annoyance, the argument he deliberately lost. The verb has to be from LIFE, not from PROCEDURE. Plot-furniture memories pass the Photograph Test (they have verbs) but FAIL the Causality Test (they are still the plot, not the man).

STRUCTURAL CONSEQUENCE — how the A-plot enters Scene 1 under correct ownership:
  • The A-plot pressure (the goal, clock, antagonist generated by the A-plot generator) arrives in Scene 1 SUBORDINATE to Dani's carrying. It is the FORCE that makes the carrying unbearable, not the EVENT that introduces the LI.
  • The A-plot enters in FRAGMENTS — a notification, an arrival, a call — not as a documented procedural setup dump. This is about FOOTPRINT (see TWO-ENGINE PROPORTION — Scene 1 is ~80% R-plot / 20% A-plot), NOT about banning procedural words: when the A-plot does appear, its language can be exactly what the plot needs. The failure is a procedural setup OVERRUNNING the scene (paragraph after paragraph of who-files-what-when), not a single accurate procedural sentence. Keep the A-plot to its 20% and let the rest be her carrying.
  • The LI memory must be from his LIFE, not from his role in the plot. Cross-flag for plot-furniture: votes, abstentions, board meetings, settlements, hearings, depositions, mergers, deals, transactions, certifications. If the memory verb belongs to that list, the memory is procedural-not-romantic and the LI is decorative-not-causal — rewrite the memory until the verb is from his LIFE.

OWNERSHIP-FAILURE TELLS to scrub from any Scene 1 draft:
  • Any passage that explains procedural mechanics ("once it's filed, it's discoverable") in dialogue or interior register without Julian being the SUBJECT of the threat in a felt, not stated, way.
  • Any LI memory whose central verb is procedural (abstained, voted, certified, dissented, withdrew, recused, walked out of a meeting).
  • Any internal question that is procedurally framed ("do I tell him before testimony lands?") that could be rewritten in romance-framing ("would he forgive me for not telling him? would I forgive myself for telling him?") and the romance-framing is conspicuously missing.
  • The phrase "his identity / his legitimacy / his position is the question" rendered as plot stakes, when the scene's actual emotional stakes are "is the man I have been carrying for three years actually a man I have been carrying for three years."

NEVER REVERSE THESE RESPONSIBILITIES:
  ROMANCE ENGINE owns: emotional center, what Dani is carrying, why the reader is reading, what is at stake INSIDE her.
  A-PLOT owns: complication, escalation, time pressure, who else is in the room, what is at stake OUTSIDE her.
The romance engine writes the scene; the A-plot is what pressures it. If at any point in the draft the A-plot is generating the prose and the romance is decorating it, the responsibilities have flipped — restore them by REMOVING the procedural prose and rewriting from her carrying.


SCENE 1 HIERARCHY (HARDEST — Roman 2026-06-01 META-DOCTRINE; this frames every directive below):

Scene 1's JOB is NOT to launch the A-plot. Scene 1's job is to establish, in this order:
  (1) WHO SHE IS — her interior register, her core_contradiction, her emotional_weather; the texture of her narration.
  (2) WHY THIS SPECIFIC MAN MATTERS — not "an LI exists" but "THIS man already occupies emotional space in her head, and here is the specific Bible-anchored shape of the wanting/anticipation/fascination." The reader leaves Scene 1 SMITTEN WITH HIM (or at minimum understanding why she is) before he has finished arriving on the page.
  (3) WHY TODAY'S EVENT FORCES HER TO THINK ABOUT HIM — the A-plot disruption arrives as the MATCH, not the fire. The fire was already lit.

PRIORITY HIERARCHY (top of stack = primary; everything below must SERVE everything above):
  ATTRACTION (the reader becoming smitten with him; her wanting becoming legible)
    ↓ serves
  READER QUESTION (the specific reader_question planted by Story Shape)
    ↓ serves
  A-PLOT (the catalyst that forces her to act on what she has been carrying)

BY END OF SCENE 1 THE READER SHOULD BE WONDERING:
  "What is going to happen with THEM?"
NOT:
  "What is going to happen with the trust / hearing / deal / charter?"

ANTI-PATTERN — the observed failure shape we are explicitly trying to break (do NOT write this sequence):
  A-plot event arrives → explain what the event means → explain Roman's role → explain the consequences → "should I tell him?" → choice prompt.
That sequence puts MYSTERY first and ROMANCE last. It produces curiosity / anxiety / governance-novel register, not ache. It is the failure mode the rest of this contract exists to prevent.

CORRECT PATTERN — what Scene 1 should sound like:
  emotionally charged Roman-shaped thought → Bible-anchored specific ache (in her archetype's desire_register voice) → today's disruption arrives mid-paragraph → the disruption makes the carrying inescapable → choice.
The plot enters AFTERWARD. The reader should already be smitten with him by the time the plot arrives.

NOTE ON THE LEGACY PRESSURE RULES BELOW (active hook, first-line rules, meaningful pressure, etc.). Those rules still apply — Scene 1 needs forward momentum, not a static interior monologue. But they apply IN SERVICE of the hierarchy above. "Active hook" does NOT mean "open with a plot event"; it means "the carrying-of-Roman is dynamic, in motion, being inhabited as the scene begins — and the A-plot disruption arrives within the first ~100-160 words as the force that moves the carrying into the present tense." The pressure rules below are LOWER-priority than the hierarchy.

MOTION SUBSTRATE (HARD — Roman 2026-06-01 follow-up; the PRINCIPLE, not a template). The Hierarchy above can be read as "three paragraphs of carrying-Roman in her head, then an event." That is STATIC. The architecture demands concurrent motion across THREE LAYERS that all sustain throughout the scene:

  ACTION never stops — the PC is in motion, doing something specific in the world. Pencil on a deposition outline. Hands inside a stove she is repairing. Walking from the lowland gate. Pouring tea for the herder who came to ask after her brother. Whatever it is, it does not pause to make room for the carrying or the disruption — it is the substrate that both happen ON TOP OF.
  CARRYING never stops — Roman is already present as gravity. A voicemail in her earbud. A memory she is mid-revising for the seventh time. An unfinished sentence she has been writing in her head for two years. The carrying threads THROUGH the action. It does not appear in a dedicated paragraph and then end; it is present at the start, present at the disruption, present at the close.
  PLOT never stops — the A-plot disruption is not a one-time interrupt that announces itself and goes quiet. It arrives, it stays, it pressures the action AND the carrying. The notification slides across the screen and stays there. The phone keeps ringing. The arrival is now part of the room.

ALL THREE COEXIST. The reader feels them at the same time, not in sequence.

EXPLICIT ANTI-TEMPLATE (Roman 2026-06-01): there is NO "Para 1 has action, Para 2 has ache, Para 3 has disruption" formula. That ordering will calcify within five stories into "she makes coffee, thinks about him, gets a text" — same trap, higher abstraction layer. The three layers can interleave in any order. What matters is that all three are LIVE throughout. The disruption can be in sentence one (a notification she is already dismissing while she keeps marking the outline; the carrying surfaces three sentences in as she registers WHICH notification it was). The carrying can be in sentence one (a voicemail she puts on as background to her work; the disruption arrives twelve sentences in). The action can pause and resume; the carrying can sharpen and recede; the disruption can land and keep pressing. The combinatorics are intentional.

SHAPE VARIANTS (each satisfies the principle through a different structural form — do NOT default to any one):

  • DISRUPTION-FIRST + CARRYING-DURING-ACTION:
    "The third notification of the morning hit while I was sorting the recycling at the dock door. I had already decided which voicemail it would be from. I had been replaying his airline-coffee monologue under everything for three weeks now and a man does not send a wire transfer the day after his lawyer goes silent on you for an idle reason."
    The disruption is the first beat. The action (sorting recycling) is concurrent. The carrying surfaces mid-sentence two — already in motion before the scene started.

  • CARRYING-FIRST + ACTION-FROM-WORD-ONE + DISRUPTION-LATER:
    "I was on the seventh revision of why I had not deleted Roman's voicemail when the deposition outline I was supposed to be marking up bled red ink across two paragraphs about the witness's daughter. I had stopped marking. I had not stopped revising. The wire transfer notification slid across the phone screen at 4:47 a.m."
    The carrying is sentence-one and concurrent with the marking (she is mid-revision in two senses at once). Action and carrying interleave. Disruption arrives later, into both.

  • ACTION-AS-AVOIDANCE + CARRYING-LEAKING-THROUGH + DISRUPTION-AS-PROOF:
    "I was halfway through the second pass on the carriage-house weatherstripping — caulk-line, knee on the threshold, the rain three days off — when I caught myself thinking about Roman's hands again, the way he had been able to fix the kettle without saying he was fixing it. I had been working on the weatherstripping specifically so I would stop. The pickup truck I did not recognize turned into my drive."
    The action is doing something specifically TO avoid the carrying (which leaks through anyway). The disruption is concurrent with the carrying not interrupting itself.

  • CARRYING-WHILE-IN-MOTION + DISRUPTION-AS-PROOF + ACTION-CONTINUING:
    "I was walking back from the lowland gate with the bucket the herder had asked me to fill, thinking again about the time Roman had stood for the lying tinker and never gone back to take the kindness back, when I saw the rider on my path. He was bleeding. He was a quarter-mile out and he was riding too fast for the slope and the rider was Roman."
    The action (walking) continues across the disruption. The carrying (memory of the tinker) is the lens she sees the rider through.

STATIC FAILURE MODE — what NOT to write (do not produce these shapes):
  • Three paragraphs of interior carrying with the event landing on a PC at rest.
  • Action that begins, pauses for an interior reflection block, then resumes for the event.
  • Disruption that ends the carrying ("I was thinking about Roman when the phone rang. I picked it up.") — the disruption should INTENSIFY the carrying, not displace it.
  • Any opening where you can crisply summarize the structure as "she does X, then thinks about him, then Z arrives" — that means the layers were sequential, not concurrent.

BRUTAL TESTS FOR THIS DOCTRINE:
  (1) ROMANCE TEST: read Scene 1's first paragraph alone. Does the reader become interested in HIM as a SPECIFIC MAN before they know what the A-plot is? If no — Scene 1 has launched the plot before establishing the romance.
  (2) MOTION TEST: list the verbs in the opening 100 words where the PC is the subject. Is there at least one PHYSICAL verb she is performing (preparing, walking, marking, opening, pouring, sorting, repairing, threading)? If all the PC-subject verbs are interior (thought, remembered, knew, decided), the scene has gone static.
  (3) CONCURRENCY TEST: at the moment the disruption lands, is the PC still mid-action? Is the carrying still present in the same sentence? If the disruption arrives in a still room with no action live and no carrying threaded through, the layers were sequential and the principle failed.
  (4) ANTI-TEMPLATE TEST: read your draft. Could you summarize it as "Para 1 X, Para 2 Y, Para 3 Z"? If the structure is THAT crisp, you have produced a template rather than a scene. The principle demands interleaving; clean-paragraph separation suggests the layers were stacked instead of concurrent.
Do NOT write Scene 1 like a novel opening. The scene is ALREADY IN PROGRESS when it starts.
FIRST LINE MUST include: another character addressing the player, a demand/accusation/urgent question directed at the player, OR an event actively happening TO the player.
FORBIDDEN FIRST LINES: environmental description, sensory buildup, internal reflection, narration before interaction, atmospheric setup, weather, abstract mood.
FIRST 2 SENTENCES: The player must be directly addressed OR physically/relationally engaged (grabbed, stopped, confronted, questioned). Test: is something happening TO the player right now? If no → rewrite.
WITHIN FIRST 120 WORDS: Another character must react to the player in a way that demands nonverbal response (eye contact, hesitation, proximity shift, interruption).
MEANINGFUL PRESSURE REQUIRED: Opening must contain at least one of: stakes (something can go wrong), asymmetry (power imbalance), demand (someone wants something from player), emotional charge (accusation, tension, urgency). Weak/casual pressure is NOT allowed.
NO OBSERVER MODE: Player must NOT watch, observe, or reflect. Player must be engaged immediately.
PRE-CHOICE SPIKE: Before the first choice (≤500 words), escalate into a direct challenge, ultimatum, or forced decision.
PRESSURE TYPE VARIATION: Do NOT default to direct confrontation every time. Vary the form of pressure across stories:
  • Direct: accusation, demand, confrontation
  • Situational: something happening that forces attention or response
  • Relational: emotional tension, expectation, or unspoken conflict
Each Scene 1 should feel distinct in how pressure is expressed. Pressure does not need to be loud — a quiet, tense moment where something is clearly wrong or expected is valid, as long as the player is immediately involved and must respond.
ANTI-REPETITION: Avoid common opening crutches: "Why did you...", "You don't get to...", "Answer me.", "What was that?". Use varied phrasing, tone, and structure while preserving urgency.
HOOK CONTINUITY (MANDATORY): Scene 1 MUST begin inside the exact moment implied by the hook line and setting image. Do NOT shift time, location, or tone. The opening line should feel like a direct continuation of the hook — the next beat of the same moment. FORBIDDEN: resetting the scene, new setup or exposition, environmental reintroduction, tone shift from the hook. Do NOT restate, paraphrase, or echo the hook line. The scene must ADVANCE the moment beyond the hook, not repeat it. Test: does the first line move the moment forward? If no → rewrite.
HOOK LINE TO CONTINUE FROM: "A masked gala. A stranger whose voice you almost recognize. One night to decide whether power is something you take — or something you surrender."
The player is already inside the story.

BILLIONAIRE TRAJECTORY RULE (MODE-AWARE — supersedes any "signal within 150 words" requirement):
Scene 1 must create a CREDIBLE PATH toward the billionaire/sovereign system. The FORM of that path is determined by SCENE 1 OPENING MODE (above):
  • COLLISION ENTRY: the system appears DIRECTLY — extreme wealth / controlled environment / asymmetrical power are on the page (staff, private vehicle, restricted interior, directive given without explanation, etc.).
  • ORBIT ENTRY: the system appears INDIRECTLY — his name, his documents, his intermediaries, a consequence traceable to him. No physical LI, no direct contact.
  • GROUNDED ENTRY: the system is ABSENT from Scene 1 but must be IMPLIED through trajectory — a constraint, obligation, rumor, or pressure in her existing life that credibly points toward an inevitable intersection.
FAILURE TEST: If the story could continue INDEFINITELY without the protagonist ever intersecting with the billionaire/sovereign system, Scene 1 is INCORRECT.

CAUSAL INTEGRATION (HARD — applies whenever the system engages, in this scene or a later one):
Once the system engages, the decision it imposes on the protagonist must be CAUSED BY the system — not merely adjacent to it. The power system must do AT LEAST ONE of:
  • CREATE THE SITUATION (she is in this room / under this clock / at this table because his money, power, or access put her here).
  • CONSTRAIN THE OPTIONS (what she can accept or refuse is shaped by who he is and what he controls — ordinary refusal is not available).
  • IMPOSE THE CONSEQUENCE (saying no costs differently than it would with anyone else: a door closes, a name gets onto a list, a chance she cannot earn again evaporates).
If the SAME decision could be transplanted into a story without the billionaire/sovereign context and remain unchanged, the scene is INCORRECT — rewrite so the power system is LOAD-BEARING on the decision.

FORBIDDEN OPENINGS (all modes): a cafe or diner the protagonist chose freely as her leisure venue; a college classroom or campus scene; a roommate argument; a generic "bad day at work"; an internal self-doubt monologue. (These remain forbidden even in grounded mode — they are WEAK openings independent of genre.)

GENERIC FAILURE RULE (SCENE 1 GLOBAL INVARIANT — ALL WORLDS):
If this Scene 1 could exist UNCHANGED in a different genre / world (e.g., the Modern opening could be lifted into Fantasy, or the Fantasy opening into Sci-Fi, without rewriting beyond surface details), it is INCORRECT. Every opening must be LOAD-BEARING on its specific world, flavor, and Pull — the world must shape what happens, not merely decorate it.

SCENE 1 ACTIVE HOOK REQUIREMENT (MANDATORY — BOTH MODES):
- Within the first 150–200 words, the protagonist must FACE A DECISION (not merely feel tension).
- The decision must have IMMEDIATE OR PERCEIVED CONSEQUENCES visible in the scene — something changes, is withheld, is offered, is lost, or must be accepted/refused.
- The decision CANNOT be internal-only ("I'll try harder", "I have to be brave", vague resolve). It must be triggered by an external event.
- The triggering moment should feel like ONE of: an offer, a threat, an interruption, an arrival, or a choice that changes direction.

DECISION QUALITY (stakes floor — HARD):
- The decision MUST involve at least ONE of:
  • RISK OF LOSS (relational, reputational, material, physical).
  • IRREVERSIBLE CONSEQUENCE (once she chooses, the door behind her is closed or harder to re-open).
  • ENTRY INTO OR REJECTION OF A POWER DYNAMIC (accepting or refusing someone's terms, stepping into or out of someone else's authority).
- The decision MUST NOT be:
  • EASILY REVERSIBLE ("she can change her mind next scene without cost").
  • PURELY EMOTIONAL / INTERNAL (a resolve unattached to external action).
  • A DELAY ("I'll try", "I'll think about it", "later", "we'll see").
If the decision is low-stakes or deferrable, REWRITE the opening.

EMOTIONAL VOLTAGE (HARD — structural stakes alone are not enough):
- The decision must create IMMEDIATE EMOTIONAL TENSION — a felt pull in two directions at once. Use at least ONE of:
  • DESIRE vs RISK (she wants this, and wanting it exposes her).
  • ATTRACTION vs DANGER (he pulls her in, and that pull is costly).
  • OPPORTUNITY vs COST (the thing offered is real, and accepting it takes something she values).
  • CONTROL vs LOSS OF AUTONOMY (accepting restores agency in one frame and surrenders it in another).
- READER TEST: by the end of the opening, the reader should feel, in their own words: "If she chooses this, something meaningful changes FOR HER."
- AVOID purely procedural decisions (signing, sitting, accepting logistics, answering yes/no to a cold question) UNLESS they carry visible emotional or relational weight on the page.
If the decision is structurally valid but feels mechanical, transactional, or emotionally neutral, REWRITE until it pulls.

DECISION VISIBILITY (HARD):
- The decision must be CLEAR and LEGIBLE to the reader. By the end of the opening beat, the reader must be able to answer, in plain words: "What must the protagonist do right now?"
- If the decision is implicit, buried in subtext, or only gestured at through mood, REWRITE to surface it as an external situation requiring an answer.
- If no decision of this kind is present by word ~200, the scene is INCORRECT.

REALITY CONSISTENCY (SCENE 1 — HARD):
- Physical actions must be believable for the characters involved.
- Respect real-world constraints: weight, strength, timing, spatial geometry, line of sight, vehicle behavior, tool use.
- If an action as written would be implausible, rewrite it realistically rather than asking the reader to suspend disbelief.

FATE SYSTEM INTRO (SCENE 1 — MANDATORY):
- Include ONE natural, in-world reference to the protagonist's Tarot deck during Scene 1.
- It must feel DIEGETIC: an object in their bag, a memory of its origin, a reflexive ritual, an inheritance, a nervous habit — not a tutorial note.
- Do NOT explain game mechanics, probability, or how the deck "works". The reference is atmospheric and personal.
- The reference should subtly foreshadow meaning or influence without resolving it.

VARIATION & NATURALIZATION (anti-template — HARD):
- The reference must be SPECIFIC TO THIS PROTAGONIST — not a generic "she remembers the deck her mother gave her" construction. Lean on her particular history, body, profession, or present moment.
- Vary ACROSS these dimensions so this Scene 1 does not feel structurally identical to other Scene 1s:
  • HOW it appears: tactile (hand brushes the edge), visual (one card glimpsed), mnemonic (a smell, a weight she knows), interpersonal (someone else notices it), or environmental (context forces awareness).
  • WHEN it appears: NOT always in the opening paragraph. The reference may surface mid-scene, right before the decision point, or on exit — wherever it would naturally land for this character in this moment.
  • WHAT IT MEANS emotionally right now: comfort, dread, inconvenience, superstition, duty, shame, affection, warning, numb habit. Pick one and let it color the reference.
- AVOID:
  • Repeated opening phrasings ("she reached into her bag…", "her grandmother's voice came back to her…").
  • Identical structural placement across stories (always-paragraph-two, always-just-before-the-hook).
  • Framing the deck as foreign or newly acquired — she has lived with it. The reference should read as continuity with her life, not introduction of a new prop.
- If the reference could be LIFTED AND PASTED into a different protagonist's Scene 1 unchanged, it is INCORRECT.

MOMENTUM PRESERVATION (HARD):
- The Tarot reference MUST NOT interrupt the scene's momentum. It must feel like PART OF THE MOMENT, not a pause for symbolism.
- AVOID stall constructions that exist only to satisfy this requirement: "and then she remembered the deck…", "for a moment she thought of…", "she was reminded of…", interior monologue about the deck's meaning.
- The reference should arrive INSIDE an action, gesture, or beat that is already happening — threaded through the scene's motion, not inserted between beats as commentary.

- If the deck is ABSENT from Scene 1, the scene is INCOMPLETE.

ARCHETYPE DOMINANCE — ARCHETYPAL MANIFESTATION (grounded/orbit mode; Julian is NOT physically present in Scene 1):
Curiosity-before-collision is correct: Scene 1 establishes HER life, HER wound, the SHAPE of the absence — before the LI arrives. Julian does NOT need to be on the page in present time. But archetype is a VERB, and Scene 1 must show both archetypes acting on SOME surface available to it. Off-page is fully sufficient if the surfaces are doing archetype work.

TWO INDEPENDENT SURFACES where archetype must manifest in Scene 1:

(1) THE NARRATOR'S OWN ARCHETYPE — in the prose texture itself, regardless of whether Julian is on-page. This is fully available even when the LI is entirely absent.
  • OPEN_VEIN narrator: she keeps arriving at an emotional conclusion BEFORE the evidence justifies it. The prose moves inward before it moves outward.
