  • When you plant the first anomaly / unanswered question, embed <<LI-MYSTERY:planted source=X>> at the END of the line where the anomaly appears, with X = relationship_mystery | behavioral_contradiction | reputation_discrepancy | missing_history | physical_marker | social_contradiction.
  • Examples (the planting prose; the marker after):
      "He held the glass a moment longer than the conversation required, then set it down. <<LI-MYSTERY:planted source=behavioral_contradiction>>"
      "There were five years no one would account for. <<LI-MYSTERY:planted source=missing_history>>"
      "When she asked why, he answered something else. <<LI-MYSTERY:planted source=relationship_mystery>>"

PLANT THE ANOMALY EARLY (Issue 1 — the reader should NOTICE without being told). Do NOT explain it this Issue. The explanation is what later Issues earn.

TURN INSTRUCTIONS:
      Story So Far: ... [PLACEHOLDER SCENE — the authored Scene 1 replaces this text.]  The invitation had no name on it, which should have been the first thing I questioned and was, instead, the reason I came. Cream stock, heavier than it needed to be. An address in a part of the city where the streetlights are spaced further apart because the people who live there prefer it that way.  I had been standing under the awning for four minutes deciding whether to go in. That is the kind of detail I keep. Not the dress, not the car that let me out, not the weather. Four minutes, and the small sound my heel made each time I shifted my weight.  Inside, the room did the thing that expensive rooms do — it made a sound like a room half its size. Masks everywhere, and beneath them the particular confidence of people who have never once had to explain themselves.  Someone said my name. Not the name on the invitation, because there wasn't one. My actual name, in a voice I was certain I had heard before and could not place, and the certainty was worse than the not-placing.  I turned around.  [PLACEHOLDER SCENE — the authored Scene 1 replaces this text.] 
      Player Action: I wait for the clerk to finish..
      Player Dialogue: Who signed this?.
      

      PLAYER INTENT ECHO (MANDATORY): Within the first 1–2 paragraphs, reference or paraphrase the player's input. Prefer character reaction or paraphrase over verbatim repetition.

      OPENING VARIATION (MANDATORY): Each scene MUST begin with a different structure, subject, and image than the previous scene.
      
═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
PICTURABILITY (HARD — top-priority craft rule; the reader must SEE these people, not only feel them).
═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

RECONCILING RULE (HARD — Roman 2026-06-09; read this first). Other directives in this prompt ban "cataloging," "clinical labels," "parts-lists," and "inert descriptors" — and they are right. Those rules ban the WRONG SHAPE of rendering; they do NOT exempt you from rendering features. DESIRE-CODED PHYSICAL SPECIFICITY IS NOT A CATALOG; IT IS THE REQUIRED RENDERING. The two rules agree: do not write the clinical list ("dark eyes, dark hair, tall"); DO write the desire-coded specificity ("those hypnotic dark eyes she had never managed to read"). If you skip features to "avoid cataloging," you have failed the picturability rule that follows. If you write a parts-list with no desire-coding, you have failed both.

DESCRIPTION QUOTA (HARD FLOOR — a MINIMUM to hit, never a budget to spend down). For each NAMED character the FIRST time they appear or are vividly evoked this scene, the prose MUST land, in the sentences around their name: (1) an OVERALL-LOOK GESTALT (how they read at a glance); AND (2) at least ONE SILHOUETTE ANCHOR — their HAIR, or their BUILD / frame / shoulders — the load-bearing detail that lets the reader assemble a BODY. Eyes-alone, a face feature alone, or a hands / behavioral tic do NOT satisfy the floor (a face is not a silhouette). A specific face feature ON TOP is welcome. {gestalt + silhouette anchor} is the floor; below it the reader cannot picture them = FAIL. For the love interest at least one anchor is DESIRE-CODED. The anti-dump SHAPE rules below cap how you DISTRIBUTE this — they NEVER reduce it below the floor. ALREADY-ESTABLISHED characters are exempt (reminder-only, see below).

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
LI — Julian (the man the protagonist wants):
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PROBLEM to avoid: Julian will be named in this scene but the reader will not be able to PICTURE him as a man she WANTS — his physical description will be missing, clinical, or thinned to one feature. That is the failure being prevented.
FIX (write this in the first draft, not in a downstream repair): at his first mention here, OR in the protagonist's memory of him, render him from his canon — these are LABELED ASSIGNMENTS, not a pool to "draw from":
  OVERALL LOOK — [object Object] · hair — [object Object] · build — 6'1", solid, deliberately still · eyes — grey-green · mouth — full, held closed · signature — the early grey at one temple
TWO LAYERS, BOTH REQUIRED:
  (1) an OVERALL-LOOK GESTALT — the holistic impression of what KIND of man / face he is and how he READS, the thing you picture FIRST. Frame it as a comparison, an effect on others, or a "face of a ___." These are SHAPES, not a phrase bank — build your own in THIS man's terms: "he had the thick, angular face of a fighter" / "in the right light, leading-man handsome and entirely unbothered by it" / "his face didn't turn heads so much as turn women toward him".
  (2) at least ONE SILHOUETTE ANCHOR — his HAIR, or his BUILD / frame / the way he fills a space — this is the LOAD-BEARING one (it lets the reader assemble a body); ON TOP of it, welcome but not sufficient alone, a specific FACE feature (eyes, the line of the jaw or mouth, brow). A gestalt with NO silhouette anchor, OR eye-colour-alone, OR a hand/fingers gesture, is NOT enough — that exact thinning is the failure being prevented.
AMOUNT: ~2 sentences on him this scene. The FLOOR is {gestalt + silhouette anchor} — land at least that. The CEILING is ~3 aspects — do not exceed it. One stray adjective, or a gestalt with no silhouette anchor, is BELOW the floor and fails.
SHAPE (HARD — anti-dump): DISTRIBUTE those 2–3 aspects across the action — his gaze, his posture, what he does, her reaction. NEVER stack 4+ traits in one uninterrupted sentence or an appositive cluster ("broad frame, dark-blonde hair thick and coarse, steel gray-green eyes, the asymmetry of his mouth…"). That pile-up is a paused character sheet, not a man she wants — it is the exact failure being prevented. The "two layers" above are a MINIMUM to be picturable, NOT a licence to inventory every canon field.
ALREADY ESTABLISHED → REMINDER ONLY: if Julian has already been pictured earlier in the story, do NOT re-render him. This scene gets ONE rotating, beat-motivated reminder (a DIFFERENT anchor than last time — voice, hands, the way he fills a space), never the full assignment list again. Re-describing the same stable features every scene is itself a dump.
REMEMBERED / OFFSTAGE (scaled DOWN, not off): if Julian is offstage or only remembered, give ONE charged physical note tied to the memory's emotional point — the one thing she cannot put down — NOT a full head-to-jaw establishment. Do not pause the live scene to assemble his face from memory; in a Scene-1 hot crisis especially, the remembered note RIDES the crisis, it does not interrupt it.

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PC — The (the first-person narrator):
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PROBLEM to avoid: The is the narrator but the reader will not be able to picture HER as desirable — her body will be invisible or rendered flat / clinical. A first-person POV does NOT exempt her from being SEEN.
FIX (write this in the first draft): in ONE in-scene moment — an action she takes, NEVER a mirror — render her look from her canon — LABELED ASSIGNMENTS, not a pool:
  OVERALL LOOK — [object Object] · signature — a chipped front tooth she never had capped · also — the hollow of her throat · hair — [object Object] · build — 5'7", narrow-shouldered, restless
TWO LAYERS, BOTH REQUIRED:
  (1) an OVERALL-LOOK GESTALT — the holistic impression of how she READS, framed as a comparison or effect, the thing seen FIRST. These are SHAPES, not a phrase bank — build your own in HER terms: "striking before she was pretty, and she had learned to use the difference" / "people said I had my mother's face, which I took as a warning, not a compliment" / "the kind of pretty that made waiters forget the order".
  (2) at least ONE SILHOUETTE ANCHOR — her HAIR, or her BUILD / frame / the line of her shoulders — the LOAD-BEARING one that lets the reader assemble a body; ON TOP of it, welcome but not sufficient alone, a specific FACE feature (eyes, the line of her jaw or mouth). A gestalt with NO silhouette anchor, OR a behavioral tic alone, is NOT enough.
SPECIAL PC RULE — render through OTHERS' EYES + DEFLECTION (never vanity): she surfaces her look through what OTHERS notice or compare her to, then DEFLECTS — she does not think she is all that special. ✓ "He noticed the first thing every man seems to notice." ✓ "I took it as a warning, not a compliment." Self-aware ease is fine; self-congratulation is NOT. BANNED: mirror clichés ("I looked at myself in the mirror"), behavioral tics as the description (ring-twisting, cuff-pulling, hair-fall — those are tells, not a face), demographic inventories ("I was a 32-year-old with brown hair").
SHE MAY occasionally WEAPONIZE a salient feature — deliberately deploy it to get her way, unsettle, or disarm someone — when the beat invites it. That is AGENCY, not vanity, and it coexists with her deflection of compliments. ✓ "I turned my hips in a way that tightened the top of the dress, pushing my breasts up in a way I knew would make him lose his words." Use sparingly, only when she has a reason to.
AMOUNT: ~2 sentences on her this scene. The FLOOR is {gestalt + silhouette anchor} — do not drop below it; do not inventory beyond it.

THIS IS A FIRST-PASS REQUIREMENT, NOT A DOWNSTREAM ENRICHMENT. The reader gets the description WHILE you are writing the scene — not from a downstream repair. If you skip these two rendering passes (LI + PC), an enforcement layer will fire post-draft to add them, which is more expensive, produces less coherent prose, and corrupts the shadow A/B comparison. Write the description into the first draft.
═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

      
WORLD PHYSICS PRIORITY (Modern / Small Town):
The selected World and Flavor define the operating physics of this scene.
Narrative elements must emerge from these world rules rather than default genre patterns.
Do NOT substitute generic genre tropes when the world flavor specifies a different structure.
Reputation, gossip networks, and social visibility must shape romantic tension.

If the generated scene does not visibly express the world's defining mechanism in paragraph one, the scene is incorrect.
Write the opening scene (900-1100 words, hard cap 1100). This is the LONGEST scene.
Establish: world, tone, power dynamics, emotional stakes, character tension.
NO sexual or romantic physical contact between the two main characters in this scene.
Ambient erotic content (side characters, memories, atmosphere) is permitted if tone is Dirty.
3-SCENE IGNITION — PHASE 1: SITUATION
This is the opening scene. Establish what physically happened or what is currently wrong.
Show the concrete situation: a ritual that failed, a relic that shattered, a battle just ended, a character arriving somewhere dangerous, a discovery, a loss.
Do NOT spend this scene on abstract emotional discussion. Ground the reader in observable reality first.
Introduce at least one new concrete fact the reader has never seen before.
FATE LANGUAGE (Modern):
When expressing omens, fate echoes, convergences, or scene gravity, use world-appropriate framing:
• Omens: a strange feeling of inevitability, a moment that lingered
• Convergence: an intuition that something important was happening
• Echoes: a memory that suddenly felt significant
• Gravity: the sense that this meeting was always going to happen
Do not use mystical language in non-fantasy worlds. Fate mechanics are constant — their narrative expression adapts to the setting.
ATTRACTION DYNAMICS:

SPARK MOMENT:
Create a brief interaction that surprises one character about the other. Highlight a trait that contradicts expectations — sarcasm showing kindness, coldness showing humor.
The reaction should be subtle: curiosity, intrigue, hesitation, or quiet fascination. Do not declare attraction yet.

ATTRACTION ANCHOR:
This relationship’s attraction source is: contrast. Characters reveal opposing traits that create intrigue or tension.
Show one observable moment that naturally creates attraction through action or behavior. Do not state attraction — show the reason first.
Output (ANCHOR_ESTABLISHED) when the moment occurs.
